It’s more just… needing to move, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and move around in those situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s like I find a reason to get up — refill coffee, look at a handout, step to the back — even when it isn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, even when they don't seem to have a purpose?\n\nPatient: Not really in a ritual sort of way. I do tap my foot or click a pen over and over when I’m sitting grading essays, or even in church. Sometimes I shift around enough that I have to tell myself to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and need to move causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not dramatic, but it does cause problems. In meetings, or even things like book club, people seem to get impatient with me. I end up feeling embarrassed afterward, like I didn’t quite fit in the way I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed when you're feeling restless and need to move?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Mostly afterward, I think. When people at work or in book club seem to lose patience with me, I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made it harder to connect with them.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually thinking about something specific when you feel the need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. It’s more like I suddenly feel I should get up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s… mostly that I have trouble staying seated in situations where I’m meant to. Meetings, dinner, that sort of thing. I’ll get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room, even when it’s not really necessary. It’s been noticeable enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're in those situations where you have trouble staying seated?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do. It’s not always dramatic, but I get this feeling that I need to be up doing something—refilling coffee, checking papers, standing at the back—when everyone else is just sitting. It’s not usually urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in those situations where you feel restless?\n\nPatient: Not especially anxious, no. At least, I haven’t really noticed it that way. It’s more just… needing to move, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and move around in those situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s like I find a reason to get up — refill coffee, look at a handout, step to the back — even when it isn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, even when they don't seem to have a purpose?\n\nPatient: Not really in a ritual sort of way. I do tap my foot or click a pen over and over when I’m sitting grading essays, or even in church. Mostly afterward, I think. When people at work or in book club seem to lose patience with me, I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made it harder to connect with them.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually thinking about something specific when you feel the need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. It’s more like I suddenly feel I should get up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. It doesn’t feel like I’m running away from a thought. It’s more just that I’m up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to get up and move around if you were alone in a room?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I notice it much more when I’m with other people. Like in meetings or at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check something, even when it’s not really needed. If I were alone… I’m not sure it would stand out as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have excess energy that you need to burn off when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe. What brought me in is more that I’ll be in a meeting or at dinner and I’m the one who gets up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room—when nothing really needs doing right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness is because you need something more engaging to think about?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I’m not sure that’s the main thing. It’s not always dramatic, but I get this feeling that I need to be up doing something—refilling coffee, checking papers, standing at the back—when everyone else is just sitting. It’s not usually urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in those situations where you feel restless?\n\nPatient: Not especially anxious, no. It’s like I find a reason to get up — refill coffee, look at a handout, step to the back — even when it isn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, even when they don't seem to have a purpose?\n\nPatient: Not really in a ritual sort of way. I do tap my foot or click a pen over and over when I’m sitting grading essays, or even in church. It’s more like I suddenly feel I should get up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. In meetings or at dinner, everyone else stays seated, and I’m up for something that… isn’t really urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to escape or get away from certain thoughts when you get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. It doesn’t feel like I’m running away from a thought. Like in meetings or at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check something, even when it’s not really needed. If I were alone… I’m not sure it would stand out as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have excess energy that you need to burn off when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe. It’s not like I’m bursting with energy exactly, but I do keep moving. What brought me in is more that I’ll be in a meeting or at dinner and I’m the one who gets up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room—when nothing really needs doing right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness is because you need something more engaging to think about?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I’m not sure that’s the main thing. It’s more that I just find myself getting up when other people are still seated—like in a meeting or at dinner. I’ll go refill coffee or check a handout or move to the back of the room, even if it’s not really urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around because your mind is not engaged enough in the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s that exactly. It’s more… I just seem to be the one getting up when everyone else is still sitting. Like at a meeting or dinner, I’ll suddenly go refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even if it’s not really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be getting up and moving around because you're not mentally challenged enough in those situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s about being mentally challenged, exactly. It’s more just that I keep getting up when everyone else is still seated—at meetings or dinner, I’ll go refill coffee or check a handout, even walk to the back—when there isn’t really a reason it has to be done then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brought me in is more that I’ll be in a meeting or at dinner and I’m the one who gets up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room—when nothing really needs doing right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness is because you need something more engaging to think about?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I’m not sure that’s the main thing. It’s more that I just find myself getting up when other people are still seated—like in a meeting or at dinner. I’ll go refill coffee or check a handout or move to the back of the room, even if it’s not really urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around because your mind is not engaged enough in the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s that exactly. It’s more… I just seem to be the one getting up when everyone else is still sitting. Like at a meeting or dinner, I’ll suddenly go refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even if it’s not really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be getting up and moving around because you're not mentally challenged enough in those situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s about being mentally challenged, exactly. It’s more just that I keep getting up when everyone else is still seated—at meetings or dinner, I’ll go refill coffee or check a handout, even walk to the back—when there isn’t really a reason it has to be done then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up and moving around is something you have to do, or is it more of a choice?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something I end up doing than a real choice. I’ll get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room, even when it’s not really necessary. It’s not always dramatic, but I get this feeling that I need to be up doing something—refilling coffee, checking papers, standing at the back—when everyone else is just sitting. It’s not usually urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in those situations where you feel restless?\n\nPatient: Not especially anxious, no. At least, I haven’t really noticed it that way. It’s more just… needing to move, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and move around in those situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s like I find a reason to get up — refill coffee, look at a handout, step to the back — even when it isn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, even when they don't seem to have a purpose?\n\nPatient: Not really in a ritual sort of way. I do tap my foot or click a pen over and over when I’m sitting grading essays, or even in church. Sometimes I shift around enough that I have to tell myself to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and need to move causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not dramatic, but it does cause problems. In meetings, or even things like book club, people seem to get impatient with me. Mostly afterward, I think. When people at work or in book club seem to lose patience with me, I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made it harder to connect with them.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually thinking about something specific when you feel the need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. It’s more like I suddenly feel I should get up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. In meetings or at dinner, everyone else stays seated, and I’m up for something that… isn’t really urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to escape or get away from certain thoughts when you get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. It doesn’t feel like I’m running away from a thought. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s… mostly that I have trouble staying seated in situations where I’m meant to. Meetings, dinner, that sort of thing. I’ll get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room, even when it’s not really necessary. It’s been noticeable enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're in those situations where you have trouble staying seated?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do. It’s not always dramatic, but I get this feeling that I need to be up doing something—refilling coffee, checking papers, standing at the back—when everyone else is just sitting. It’s not usually urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in those situations where you feel restless?\n\nPatient: Not especially anxious, no. At least, I haven’t really noticed it that way. It’s more just… needing to move, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and move around in those situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s like I find a reason to get up — refill coffee, look at a handout, step to the back — even when it isn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, even when they don't seem to have a purpose?\n\nPatient: Not really in a ritual sort of way. I do tap my foot or click a pen over and over when I’m sitting grading essays, or even in church. Sometimes I shift around enough that I have to tell myself to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and need to move causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not dramatic, but it does cause problems. In meetings, or even things like book club, people seem to get impatient with me. I end up feeling embarrassed afterward, like I didn’t quite fit in the way I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed when you're feeling restless and need to move?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Mostly afterward, I think. When people at work or in book club seem to lose patience with me, I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made it harder to connect with them.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually thinking about something specific when you feel the need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. It’s more like I suddenly feel I should get up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. In meetings or at dinner, everyone else stays seated, and I’m up for something that… isn’t really urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to escape or get away from certain thoughts when you get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. It doesn’t feel like I’m running away from a thought. It’s more just that I’m up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to get up and move around if you were alone in a room?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I notice it much more when I’m with other people. Like in meetings or at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check something, even when it’s not really needed. If I were alone… I’m not sure it would stand out as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have excess energy that you need to burn off when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe. It’s not like I’m bursting with energy exactly, but I do keep moving. Since my youngest left for college and the house is quieter, I’ll go from lesson planning to laundry to something for softball or chopping food for a community event… even on nights I’d told myself I was going to rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around because you're bored with what's going on?\n\nPatient: Maybe sometimes, but I don’t think it’s just boredom. What brought me in is more that I’ll be in a meeting or at dinner and I’m the one who gets up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room—when nothing really needs doing right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness is because you need something more engaging to think about?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I’m not sure that’s the main thing. It’s more that I just find myself getting up when other people are still seated—like in a meeting or at dinner. I do tap my foot or click a pen over and over when I’m sitting grading essays, or even in church. It’s more like I suddenly feel I should get up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. It doesn’t feel like I’m running away from a thought. It’s more just that I’m up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to get up and move around if you were alone in a room?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I notice it much more when I’m with other people. Like in meetings or at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check something, even when it’s not really needed. It’s not like I’m bursting with energy exactly, but I do keep moving. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

Yes… I think so, in a way. Since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I just keep moving from one thing to the next—lesson planning, laundry, softball practice, chopping food for some community thing. Even on nights I tell myself I’m going to rest, I don’t really stop.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s… mostly that I have trouble staying seated in situations where I’m meant to. Meetings, dinner, that sort of thing. I’ll get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room, even when it’s not really necessary. It’s been noticeable enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're in those situations where you have trouble staying seated?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do. It’s not always dramatic, but I get this feeling that I need to be up doing something—refilling coffee, checking papers, standing at the back—when everyone else is just sitting. It’s not usually urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in those situations where you feel restless?\n\nPatient: Not especially anxious, no. At least, I haven’t really noticed it that way. It’s more just… needing to move, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to get up and move around in those situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s like I find a reason to get up — refill coffee, look at a handout, step to the back — even when it isn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, even when they don't seem to have a purpose?\n\nPatient: Not really in a ritual sort of way. I do tap my foot or click a pen over and over when I’m sitting grading essays, or even in church. Sometimes I shift around enough that I have to tell myself to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of restlessness and need to move causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not dramatic, but it does cause problems. In meetings, or even things like book club, people seem to get impatient with me. I end up feeling embarrassed afterward, like I didn’t quite fit in the way I meant to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed when you're feeling restless and need to move?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Mostly afterward, I think. When people at work or in book club seem to lose patience with me, I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made it harder to connect with them.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually thinking about something specific when you feel the need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. It’s more like I suddenly feel I should get up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. In meetings or at dinner, everyone else stays seated, and I’m up for something that… isn’t really urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to escape or get away from certain thoughts when you get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. It doesn’t feel like I’m running away from a thought. It’s more just that I’m up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to get up and move around if you were alone in a room?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I notice it much more when I’m with other people. Like in meetings or at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check something, even when it’s not really needed. If I were alone… I’m not sure it would stand out as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have excess energy that you need to burn off when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe. It’s not like I’m bursting with energy exactly, but I do keep moving. Since my youngest left for college and the house is quieter, I’ll go from lesson planning to laundry to something for softball or chopping food for a community event… even on nights I’d told myself I was going to rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around because you're bored with what's going on?\n\nPatient: Maybe sometimes, but I don’t think it’s just boredom. What brought me in is more that I’ll be in a meeting or at dinner and I’m the one who gets up—refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room—when nothing really needs doing right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness is because you need something more engaging to think about?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I’m not sure that’s the main thing. It’s more that I just find myself getting up when other people are still seated—like in a meeting or at dinner. I’ll go refill coffee or check a handout or move to the back of the room, even if it’s not really urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you get up and move around because your mind is not engaged enough in the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s that exactly. It’s more… I just seem to be the one getting up when everyone else is still sitting. Like at a meeting or dinner, I’ll suddenly go refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even if it’s not really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be getting up and moving around because you're not mentally challenged enough in those situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s about being mentally challenged, exactly. It’s more just that I keep getting up when everyone else is still seated—at meetings or dinner, I’ll go refill coffee or check a handout, even walk to the back—when there isn’t really a reason it has to be done then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like getting up and moving around is something you have to do, or is it more of a choice?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something I end up doing than a real choice. I’ll suddenly think I should refill coffee or check a handout, or just go to the back of the room… even when it could easily wait.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less restless if you were more mentally engaged in the activities you're doing?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I’m not sure. What I’ve noticed is just that I keep getting up when other people stay seated—like in meetings or at dinner. I’ll go refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even if it’s not really urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less likely to get up and move around if the activities you were in were more intellectually stimulating?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Maybe a little, but what I’ve noticed is more that I just get up anyway—meetings, dinner, that kind of thing. I’ll suddenly go refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back, even when it’s not actually urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to get up and move around because your body feels like it needs to be active?\n\nPatient: I suppose it does feel that way sometimes. I don’t always think of it as my body “needing” it, but I keep finding reasons to get up—refill coffee, check something, walk to the back—even when everyone else is still seated and it isn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body is driving you to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, in a way. Since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I just keep moving from one thing to the next—lesson planning, laundry, softball practice, chopping food for some community thing. Even on nights I tell myself I’m going to rest, I don’t really stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
